    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

|\\n\\n## 重要发现\\nevaluate.py 的 \\\"Dense baseline\\\" 实际是通过 predictor hard mask 的评估（PredictorWrapper._patch_mlps() monkey-patch 所有 MLP，forward_dense() 执行后 sparse_mode 重置为 True）。因此 \\\"dense\\\" PPL neuron。\\n\\n## Per-layer sparsity 分布差异\\n- KL: 非均匀 (0.215-0.806), 中间层低稀疏(保护), 后层高稀疏\\n- BCE: 较均匀 (0.430-0.664), 后层略高\\n\\n## 结论: SUCCESS\\nKL normalized loss 在所有指标上显著优于 BCE: TEAL sparse PPL 12.08 vs <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me analyze the findings:

1. **GPU compliance**: 
   - Constraint C001: Only cuda:0 and cuda:1 are allowed
   - `spon_2048_s42` uses `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0` → cuda:0 ✅
   - Only PID 109606 is on GPU (33908 MiB on GPU 0) ✅
   - No GPU boundary violation

2. **Training process**: PID 109606 is actively running (89.4% CPU, 2.3GB RAM, 33908 MiB GPU memory). Training just started.
   
3. **STE baseline discrepancy**: The `ste_baseline_s42` experiment shows ALLOCATED status on cuda:1 with a start time of 18:39:42, but GPU 1 is completely idle—0% utilization and no memory in use. The registry entry indicates it was allocated and started, but there's no actual process running on the GPU, which is odd.

Looking at the registry data more carefully, I see `ste_baseline_s42` has a `reproduce` entry with server and GPU info but no PID recorded. Meanwhile, the worker that just finished (`worker_exp_spon_2048_s42_20260515T184006`) was responsible for launching the SPON seq_len=2048 experiment, which now shows as PREPARING with PID 109601 and a submission timestamp of 18:41:46.

The worker successfully submitted the training job and it's running on cuda:0 as expected. The `ste_baseline_s42` allocation on cuda:1 appears to be a separate scheduling issue since its worker hasn't been dispatched yet, so that's not critical to flag right now.

Looking at what the worker executed, it killed the old `spon_training_seq2048` experiment with the train/eval sequence length mismatch and relaunched it as `spon_2048_s42` with the corrected parameters. The configuration looks solid—seq_len is now uniformly 2048, batch size was appropriately reduced to 4 to accommodate the longer sequences, it's using the KL-normalized predictor checkpoint, and gradient checkpointing is enabled to handle the memory requirements. All the other hyperparameters like learning rate, step count, and seed are consistent with the plan.

This is a critical test for Claim 2 since the original SPON training only achieved 3.8% recovery at seq_len=128. If this rerun with matching sequence lengths still shows minimal improvement, it strengthens the argument that KL subsumes SPON rather than the mismatch being the culprit. Conversely, if we see meaningful gains here, it suggests the original negative result was confounded by the train/eval discrepancy.

The training job has successfully submitted and is now running on the server with the correct parameters—GPU allocation checks out, the process is active with substantial resource usage, though the registry status hasn't updated to RUNNING yet. I should verify whether the other baseline job actually has a tmux session running or if it was never properly launched.

Looking at the registry more carefully, `ste_baseline_s42` shows as ALLOCATED with a start timestamp but lacks the command, PID, and submission details that would indicate an actual training job was launched—it's just reserved the GPU without actually running anything. This means cuda:1 is sitting idle while the primary experiment runs on cuda:0, which is a scheduling inefficiency but not a compliance problem.

Now I'm ready to finalize my assessment: the spon_2048_s42 worker passes compliance since training launched correctly on cuda:0 and the parameters properly address the sequence length confound, though the registry status should update automatically and there's that unused GPU capacity to note in my review.
```
